My neighbor's son had his identity stolen last year, and it took him months to sort it out with the credit bureaus. He had to freeze all his accounts, call the police, just a complete nightmare. I would hate to deal with that kind of mess on top of everything else. \n\nBut... oh, what were you asking? Right, the control. Yeah, no, it's just the food. Because with the food... once I start, there is this awful switch that flips. I can literally be sitting there thinking, Taylor, put it away, and my hands still keep opening containers like I am not the one in charge.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. The guilt is... it's just crushing. I'll sit there looking at all the empty containers and just feel so disgusted with myself. I mean, I know better. I'm a smart woman. I have an associate's degree in business administration, which took me four years to get because I was working full-time at the dealership while taking night classes. That was exhausting, let me tell you. I'd get off work at five, drive across town to the community college, and sit through three hours of accounting or business law. My professor for business law, Mr. Henderson, he was a piece of work. He used to wear these terrible plaid suits from the seventies, and he sweat profusely, just constantly dabbing his forehead with this yellow handkerchief. We used to take bets in the back row on how many times he'd pull it out during a lecture. I think the record was twenty-two times. It was so distracting. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the shame. Yeah, it's really bad. I'll just sit there and cry sometimes because I don't understand why I can't just stop like a normal person.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of losing control and eating a large amount of food happen?\n\nPatient: Well, I actually keep a journal. I started it a few years ago because my niece, Sarah, got me this beautiful leather-bound notebook for my birthday. She's so thoughtful, that girl. She's studying art history over in Europe right now—spending a whole semester in Florence. Can you imagine? I would love to go to Italy. The pasta, the architecture, just sitting at a little cafe in a piazza somewhere. But the flights are just so expensive, and honestly, the thought of sitting on a plane for nine hours makes my back ache just thinking about it. I have this terrible lower back pain from sitting at my desk all day. I bought one of those ergonomic chairs, the ones that cost a small fortune, but I don't think it really helps. \n\nBut anyway, I was looking back through that journal recently. And... gosh, it's a lot. There has barely been a week in the last several months without at least one of these nights. Honestly, most weeks, it happens several times. I'll just write it down in the journal, almost like a confession, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, fast doesn't even begin to cover it. I'll usually just be standing there at the dining room table... which, by the way, is this gorgeous solid mahogany piece I found at an estate sale over in Westlake a few years back. You just don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Everything nowadays is that cheap particle board you have to put together yourself with a tiny little hex wrench. My nephew, Brian, bought an entire bedroom set from one of those flat-pack places when he moved into his first apartment, and I swear, half the drawers don't even open right. He's an engineering student, too, so you'd think he'd be able to assemble a simple dresser, but it took him three days and a lot of swearing. I told him he should have just gone to a real furniture store, but you know kids these days, they want everything cheap and delivered right to their door. \n\nBut anyway... I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. What were we talking about? Oh, right, the speed. Yes. When I'm having one of those nights, I eat so fast that I barely even taste anything. I'm just standing there at that mahogany table with the microwave running and boxes all around me, and I'm swallowing one bite while I am literally already reaching for the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're less interested in doing things you normally enjoy after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it definitely puts a damper on things. I used to love having people over, you know? I'd host these little dinner parties. I have this wonderful recipe for chicken piccata that my mother taught me. She was an incredible cook. She grew up during the Depression, so she knew how to stretch a dollar, but she could make the most basic ingredients taste like a five-star meal. We always had a home-cooked dinner on the table at six o'clock sharp, every single night. Even when she was working full-time at the department store downtown—the old Macy's, before it closed down and they turned the building into those luxury lofts. The lofts are nice, I suppose, with the exposed brick and all, but the rent is just astronomical. Who can afford three thousand dollars a month for a one-bedroom apartment? It's ridiculous. \n\nBut, um, what was I saying? Oh, right, the dinner parties. Yeah, I've stopped doing that. I just don't invite people over anymore. And I've been skipping my evening yoga, too. I used to go to this lovely studio on Elm Street, but after one of these episodes, I just feel so ashamed and... heavy, I guess. I can't even bring myself to do my gardening chores, and my hydrangeas are really suffering for it. I'm just too preoccupied with how awful I feel to do any of the things I usually enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before one of these episodes where you lose control and eat a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. A lot of the time I'm not physically hungry at all. I mean, I might have just had a perfectly normal dinner. Like last night, I made a lovely piece of salmon with some asparagus. I get my salmon from this little fish market down on 4th Street. The owner, Sal, he's been there for thirty years. He always saves the best cuts for his regulars. His wife, Maria, she makes this incredible homemade tartar sauce that they sell in little jars by the register. I keep telling her she should bottle it and sell it at the grocery store, but she says it's too much work. They're thinking about retiring soon and moving to Florida. My sister moved to Florida a few years ago—Clearwater, right by the beach. The humidity down there is just terrible for my hair, though. I went to visit her last July and I swear I looked like a poodle the entire week. Plus, the bugs! They have these palmetto bugs that are basically just giant flying cockroaches. I don't know how she deals with it. \n\nBut anyway... where was I? Oh, the hunger. Right. So, even if I've had a full dinner, like that salmon, later on I'll still find myself just... looking. I'll go digging through the fridge or the pantry shelves, just pulling things out. It's almost like the food is a chore I have to finish, you know? Even if my stomach is completely full, I just keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're watching yourself eat, but you're not really in control, like you're detached from what's happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly what it feels like. It's like a switch flips. Speaking of switches, we actually just had the electrical wiring redone at the office—I supervise the administrative support team over at a logistics firm downtown. Let me tell you, the contractors the building manager hired were just a nightmare. They kept flipping the main breakers without giving us any warning, so half my staff would lose all the data they were entering. I finally had to march down there and have a very stern talk with the foreman. I mean, you'd think professionals would have a little more common sense, right? It's just like when I hired that plumber for my guest bathroom last spring and he ended up flooding the hallway carpet... \n\nOh, I'm so sorry, I'm getting completely off track again, aren't I? What were you asking? Oh, feeling detached, right. \n\nYes. Once I start, there is this awful switch that flips. I can be standing there, completely aware, thinking to myself, *Taylor, put it away, just stop*, but my hands just keep opening containers anyway. It's terrifying, honestly, because it feels exactly like I am not the one in charge.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these episodes of losing control and eating a large amount of food usually happen when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset about something?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure if it's always tied to feeling upset. I mean, sometimes I'm stressed, sure. Work has been incredibly stressful lately. We're rolling out this new software system for all the administrative staff, and it is just a disaster. The vendor promised us it would be seamless, but it's full of bugs. My team is constantly calling me over to fix things, and I have to put in a ticket with IT every five minutes. The IT guy, Kevin, he's a sweetheart, but he's so overworked. He just had his first baby, a little girl. He showed me pictures the other day, and she is just precious. They named her Lily. I always loved that name. If I had had a daughter, I think I would have named her Lily, or maybe Rose. I've always been partial to flower names. My grandmother's name was Violet. She was the one who taught me how to garden, actually. She had this incredible green thumb. She could make anything grow... \n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, right, stress and eating. Um, I don't know, honestly. Sometimes I'm stressed, but other times I could just be sitting at home on a perfectly calm Sunday afternoon, and it still happens. So I haven't really noticed a clear connection like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're trying to escape or avoid any feelings when you find yourself eating like this?\n\nPatient: Well, maybe? I mean, I don't really know if I'm trying to escape anything. I guess sometimes I just want to zone out. You know how it is when you just want to turn your brain off for a minute? I used to do that by watching those home renovation shows. I was obsessed with this one couple who flipped houses in Texas. They had the most beautiful farmhouse style. I actually tried to recreate that look in my own living room a couple of years ago. I bought this distressed wood coffee table and these big, chunky knit throw blankets. I even painted the walls this very specific shade of grayish-blue. It took me three trips to the hardware store to get the color right. The guy at the paint counter, Hector, he was so patient with me. He mixed like five different samples. He was telling me about how he's trying to save up to buy his own house, which is just so hard these days with the interest rates being what they are. It's ridiculous, honestly. How are young people supposed to afford anything? \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Escaping feelings? I don't know, I really don't. Like I said, I don't think I'm running away from anything specific. I just... I just end up doing it, and I don't really know why.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself or feel guilty after these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Guilty? I don't know, I haven't really noticed feeling that way. I mean, guilt is such a heavy thing. My mother, bless her heart, she was the absolute queen of guilt trips. If I didn't call her by exactly 2 PM on a Sunday, she would answer the phone sounding like she was on her deathbed. I actually missed her call one weekend because I was at this huge antique fair down by the lake. They hold it every October, and it is just massive. I found this gorgeous vintage desk lamp there—solid brass with a green glass shade, kind of like the ones you see in old libraries? The dealer wanted two hundred dollars for it, but I managed to haggle him down to a hundred and twenty-five. I was so proud of myself! I love a good bargain. My friend Susan and I try to go to all the estate sales in our area on Saturday mornings. You wouldn't believe the things people just give away or sell for pennies. Susan found a pristine set of silver flatware last month... \n\nWait, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so sorry. You were asking about feeling guilty after I eat. Um, no, I don't think I really criticize myself or feel guilty. I just haven't really noticed feeling like that. It's more like it just happens and then it's over.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Weight changes? Um, no, I haven't really noticed anything significant. I mean, my weight kind of just stays around the same place. It's funny you ask about weight, though, because my sister, Diane, she's been on this crazy new diet. It's one of those where you only eat meat? Like, literally just steak and butter. She swears by it, says she has all this energy now. But I don't know, it just sounds so heavy. I prefer a nice salad, especially in the summer. We actually started a little community garden in our neighborhood last spring. A bunch of us got together and cleared out this empty lot that the city wasn't using. It was so much work pulling up all the weeds—my back ached for a week! But it was worth it. We planted tomatoes, zucchini, cucumbers... oh, and the most beautiful sunflowers. They got to be like eight feet tall! The neighborhood kids loved them. There's this one little boy, Tommy, who lives two doors down from me, and he would come over every afternoon just to measure how much the sunflowers had grown. He's such a bright kid, always asking questions about how things work. His mom is a nurse at the county hospital, so she works these crazy long shifts...\n\nWait, sorry, I completely lost my train of thought. Weight changes, right. Yeah, no, I haven't really noticed my weight going up or down much. It's just pretty steady, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by a specific emotional state, such as feeling sad, anxious, or bored?\n\nPatient: Emotional triggers? Um, I'm not really sure, to be honest. I don't think I feel particularly sad or anxious before it happens. I mean, everyone gets a little stressed sometimes, right? Like at the office lately, it's been a bit of a madhouse. I supervise a team of admin staff, and upper management just rolled out this completely new scheduling software without really training us on it first. My best girl, Brenda, she's usually so quick to pick things up, but she's been so distracted because her youngest daughter is planning a wedding. Oh, my goodness, weddings these days! Brenda was showing me the quotes they got for the floral arrangements, and I nearly fell right out of my chair. Thousands of dollars just for some peonies and eucalyptus! When I got married back in the nineties, we just used silk flowers from the craft store and it looked perfectly fine. We had the reception at the local VFW hall, and my Uncle Arthur played the accordion. We danced the polka until midnight! It was so much fun, and nobody had to take out a second mortgage to pay for it. People just expect too much now, don't they? Everything has to look like it belongs on a Pinterest board... \n\nOh, goodness, listen to me go on. I am so sorry, I do this all the time. You were asking if I eat because I'm bored or sad? Yeah, I really don't think so. I haven't noticed any specific mood that sets it off. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're criticizing yourself after these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Criticizing myself? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, we just talked about guilt, right? It's kind of the same thing. I really try not to be too hard on myself in general. Life is just too short to constantly be picking yourself apart. That's what I always tell my niece, Emily. She's in her second year of college down at State, and she is just so incredibly hard on herself. If she gets an A-minus instead of an A, she practically locks herself in her dorm room for a weekend. I told her, \"Emily, honey, ten years from now, no one is going to ask you what grade you got in Intro to Sociology.\" I actually took a sociology class when I was getting my associate's degree, and it was fascinating. Yeah, no, I don't really do that. I just don't think about it much afterwards.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by a specific emotional state?\n\nPatient: Wait, didn't we... didn't you just ask me that? About being sad or bored or whatever? I'm pretty sure we just talked about this, right? Like I said, I don't really think there's a specific mood that sets it off. I don't feel particularly anxious or anything. I mean, speaking of feeling anxious, my neighbor's dog, this little terrier mix named Buster, he gets so anxious whenever it thunderstorms. It breaks my heart. Last week we had that huge storm roll through—did you hear the thunder? It shook the windows! Anyway, poor Buster dug a hole right under their fence and ended up in my backyard, hiding under the patio furniture. I had to coax him out with a piece of ham. He was shaking like a leaf. Dogs just don't understand thunder, do they? It must sound like the end of the world to them. My old cat, Whiskers, he didn't care about storms at all. He would just sleep right through them on the back of the sofa...\n\nBut anyway, yeah, I'm pretty sure we just covered the emotional trigger thing. I still don't really think that's it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you feel better when you're upset or stressed?\n\nPatient: Feel better? Um, I don't really know. I don't think I've ever really paid attention to whether it makes me feel better or not. I guess I just eat, you know? It's not like I'm trying to fix a bad mood. Speaking of fixing things, though, my husband has been trying to fix the leaky faucet in the guest bathroom for three weeks now. I keep telling him, \"Frank, just call a plumber,\" but he insists he can do it himself. He watched a YouTube video and bought all these special wrenches from the hardware store. But every time we turn the water back on, it drips even worse than before! It's driving me absolutely up the wall. The sound of it—drip, drip, drip—all night long. It reminds me of that time we stayed in this little bed and breakfast in Vermont for our anniversary. Beautiful place, gorgeous fall foliage, but our room was right next to a gutter that was clogged with leaves, and it rained the entire weekend. We barely slept a wink! We ended up leaving a day early just to get some peace and quiet in our own bed. \n\nOh, goodness, I'm so sorry. I'm rambling again, aren't I? Frank always says I could talk the ear off a brass monkey. But to answer your question, no, I don't really think eating is about making myself feel better when I'm stressed. I just haven't noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating is a way for you to deal with or manage your emotions?\n\nPatient: Manage my emotions? Gosh, I feel like I'm repeating myself, but no, I really don't think so. I mean, we just talked about the whole stress and mood thing, right? I just don't see it as a way to deal with feelings. Dealing with feelings is... well, it's complicated, isn't it? My sister, Linda, she's a crier. Anything happens, she just bursts into tears. We were at the grocery store last Tuesday, just picking up a few things for a potluck at work—I'm the office manager at a logistics company, and organizing these potlucks is practically a full-time job in itself. Getting people to sign up for something other than chips and soda is like pulling teeth! Anyway, we're in the produce aisle, and they were completely out of those little cocktail cucumbers she likes, and I swear, her eyes just welled right up. Over cucumbers! I told her, \"Linda, they're just vegetables, we'll get the regular ones and slice them,\" but she was just so overwhelmed. She's been under a lot of stress lately with her kitchen remodel. The contractors found water damage behind the cabinets, and it's turning into this whole huge ordeal... \n\nOh, I'm doing it again. I am so sorry. You asked about eating to manage emotions. No, I really don't think that's what's going on with me. I just don't see a connection there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you avoid thinking about your problems or difficult emotions?\n\nPatient: Avoid thinking about problems? No, I... I don't think so. I mean, we've kind of been circling this same idea, haven't we? About feelings and eating. I really don't think I'm using food to hide from my problems. If anything, I think too much about my problems! Like right now, I cannot stop thinking about the registration for my car. The DMV sent the renewal notice to my old address—even though I updated it online months ago, I swear I did. I have the confirmation email printed out in a folder on my desk. I'm very organized like that, you have to be in my line of work. But anyway, the notice went to the old house, and the new owners, the Millers—lovely couple, they have twins—they didn't forward it. So now my tags are expired, and I'm terrified I'm going to get pulled over on my way to work. I even had a dream about it last night. I just don't see it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Unable to stop? Yes... yes, exactly that. It's terrifying, honestly. Once I start, there is this... this awful switch that flips. I can literally be standing there thinking, *Taylor, put it away, just stop*, but my hands just keep opening containers. It’s like I'm not even the one in charge of my own body. 

Which is so frustrating, because normally I am very much in charge of the containers! Just last week, I completely overhauled the cupboards in the office breakroom. People were hoarding all those flimsy takeout boxes—you know, the black plastic ones with the clear lids? They get completely warped in the dishwasher, and they just look so tacky. Plus, someone had left one full of leftover tilapia in the back of the fridge for God knows how long. The smell was absolutely atrocious! I had to prop the breakroom door open and run that little oscillating desk fan I keep by my computer just to air the place out. I ended up driving to Costco on my lunch break to buy a proper set of glass storage bowls with the snap-on lids. I love Costco, but let me tell you, the parking lot on a Tuesday afternoon is surprisingly cutthroat. This woman in a silver minivan completely stole my spot while I had my blinker on and was waiting for a family to back out. She just zipped right in! I honked, but she didn't even look at me. 

Oh, gosh. Listen to me. I took a total left turn there, didn't I? I'm so sorry. But yes... to answer your question, when I'm in the middle of it, I just can't stop. I tell myself to stop, but my hands keep going and I am just not in control at all.
